Improved weak convergence for the long time simulation of Mean-field Langevin equations
Xingyuan Chen, Gonçalo dos Reis, Wolfgang Stockinger, Zac Wilde

Abstract




Abstract.

We study the weak convergence behaviour of the Leimkuhler–Matthews method, a non-Markovian Euler-type scheme with the same computational cost as the Euler scheme, for the approximation of the stationary distribution of a one-dimensional McKean–Vlasov Stochastic Differential Equation (MV-SDE). The particular class under study is known as mean-field (overdamped) Langevin equations (MFL).
We provide weak and strong error results for the scheme in both finite and infinite time.
We work under a strong convexity assumption.

Based on a careful analysis of the variation processes and the Kolmogorov backward equation for the particle system associated with the MV-SDE, we show that the method attains a higher-order approximation accuracy in the long-time limit (of weak order convergence rate
3/2
) than the standard Euler method (of weak order
1
).
While we use an interacting particle system (IPS) to approximate the MV-SDE, we show the convergence rate is independent of the dimension of the IPS and this includes establishing uniform-in-time decay estimates for moments of the IPS, the Kolmogorov backward equation and their derivatives. The theoretical findings are supported by numerical tests.

Key words.
Weak error, higher-order schemes, non-Markovian Euler, stationary distribution, mean-field Langevin equations

AMS subject classifications.

65C30, 60H35, 37M25

1
Introduction

In this paper, for
t\geq 0
, we consider a class of McKean–Vlasov Stochastic Differential Equations (MV-SDE), specifically the one-dimensional mean-field Langevin (MFL) equation:

\displaystyle X_{t}=\xi-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\nabla U(X_{s})+\nabla V*\mu_{s}(X_{s})\Big)\mathrm{d}s+\sigma W_{t},

(1.1)

where
\mu_{t}
is the law of
X_{t}
,
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and
\xi\in L^{p}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
for some given
p\geq 2
(i.e. the initial state is an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable random variable with finite
p
-th moments). Following a statistical physics interpretation
[
15
]
, the map
U:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is labelled the confining potential and
V:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
the interaction potential.
*
denotes the usual convolution operator given by
(f*\mu)(\cdot)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\cdot-y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)
for some given integrable function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Of particular interest is the equation’s stationary distribution
\mu^{*}

and specifically how to efficiently generate samples from it. The latter problem has garnered additional interest due to its role in the study of training neural networks (via stochastic gradient descent algorithms) in the mean-field regime

[
51
,
27
,
25
,
24
,
46
,
36
]
.
We consider the case where the functions
U,V
satisfy suitable regularity and convexity assumptions (Assumption
1
), thus the process described in (
1.1
) admits a unique stationary distribution
\mu^{*}
(e.g.,
[
15
,
22
]
) with a well-known implicit form satisfying

\displaystyle\mu^{*}(x)\propto\exp\Big(-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}U(x)-\frac{2}{\sigma^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}V(x-y)\mu^{*}(\mathrm{d}y)\Big).

(1.2)

The mainstream method to sample from
\mu^{*}
is to simulate the weakly-interacting
N
-particle SDE system (IPS) that approximates (
1.1
) in the mean-field limit. That is, the law of the solution to (
1.1
) is approximated, for some sufficiently large
N
, by the empirical distribution associated with the IPS
(\bm{X}^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\coloneqq(X_{t}^{1,N},\ldots,X_{t}^{N,N})_{t\geq 0}
with components defined for
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
by

\displaystyle X_{t}^{i,N}

\displaystyle=\xi^{i}-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\nabla U(X^{i,N}_{s})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\nabla V(X^{i,N}_{s}-X^{j,N}_{s})\Big)\mathrm{d}s+\sigma W^{i}_{t},

(1.3)

where
(\xi^{i},W^{i})_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}
are a collection of i.i.d. copies of
(\xi,W)
.
We briefly review some important results providing quantitative convergence guarantees for the approximation of (
1.1
) through its IPS.
Broadly speaking, the error of approximating (
1.1
) by an
N
-particle system (
1.3
) has been widely studied in the literature of Propagation of Chaos (PoC), stemming from the seminal works
[
53
,
47
]
. In a nutshell, the accuracy of the
N
-particle approximation is known to behave like
\mathcal{O}(1/N)
in the
squared
L^{2}
-norm or squared
W^{(2)}
-Wasserstein metric
(under Lipschitz type assumptions on the interaction and confining potentials).
These quantitative convergence results are often referred to as strong PoC
[
53
,
47
,
11
]
.
More recently,
[
40
, Theorem 2.2]
shows that the PoC convergence rate for models of the form (
1.1
) (in relative entropy and finite time) can be improved from
\mathcal{O}(1/N)
to
\mathcal{O}(1/N^{2})
(under certain smallness conditions), with
[
40
, Example 2.8]
showing that the rate is optimal. We refer the reader to the introductions of the papers
[
40
,
41
]
and review articles
[
16
,
17
]
for a holistic discussion on this topic.

When the PoC holds up to
T=\infty
, e.g.,
[
15
,
41
]
, it is called
uniform in time PoC
– see our Proposition
2.1
for a formulation in the strong sense. Uniform in time PoC results for the model (
1.1
) have received significant attention in the last few years; see e.g.
[
41
,
21
,
30
,
15
]
and references cited therein.

Another string of interests in terms of quantitative PoC results, is the weak convergence case. In the context of quantitative weak PoC results (i.e. the absolute difference between expectations), we refer to the recent work
[
18
]
establishing finite-time higher order weak PoC results via techniques from differential calculus on the space of measures along with the study of Kolmogorov backward PDEs written on the Wasserstein space.
Also,
[
35
, Theorem 3.1]
establishes a finite-time weak PoC result of rate
\mathcal{O}(1/N)
based on a more classical approach, via a Talay–Tubaro expansion and an analysis of a Kolmogorov backward PDE associated with the whole IPS. Both
[
18
,
35
]
establish their results for
T<\infty
.

To the best of our knowledge, the only quantitative uniform-in-time weak PoC results that apply to our setting is the very recent
[
8
, Theorem 1.1]
. From a methodological perspective
[
8
]
is close to
[
18
]
(for
T<\infty
) and somewhat close to
[
28
]
(we note that
[
28
]
obtains weak uniform-in-time PoC results but over the torus and draws on the Fokker-Planck equation).
Loosely, the result states that the accuracy of the
N
-particle approximation to
\mu_{t}
behaves like
\mathcal{O}(1/N)
uniformly in time over a metric akin to the
W^{(1)}
-Wasserstein metric.

Once (
1.3
) and a PoC rate is established, the sampling from
\mu^{*}
(
1.2
) is obtained via discretization of (
1.3
) with a convenient numerical scheme (also called numerical integrator or sampler).
There is a growing body of contributions on the topic of sampling from the (overdamped) MFL stationary distribution using this method
[
52
,
39
]
. Although said works provide a variety of quantitative PoC rate type results (under a variety of conditions),
the time discretization schemes used are all of Euler type
– see
[
52
, Theorem 3 and 4]
or
[
39
, Section 4]
– the final error rates or sampling guarantees encase a leading order
1
dependence on the time-discretization stepsize. We also mention two recent contributions on unadjusted Hamiltonian Monte Carlo for the simulation of the (underdamped or kinetic) MFL
[
12
,
13
]
. The main emphasis of the present work is to improve the weak convergence order (to the stationary distribution) of the standard Euler scheme using a non-Markovian version of it.

We briefly mention some (recent) contributions on numerical schemes for MV-SDEs. The seminal works
[
10
,
11
]
investigate the convergence rates of the particle system’s and Euler scheme’s approximation accuracy of the cumulative distribution (in
L^{1}
-norm) for the Burger’s type MV-SDE using density estimates or using a Malliavin calculus approach
[
2
]
.
In the context of finite time-horizon simulation there are many recent contributions (e.g., Euler and Milstein schemes) focusing on the approximation error stemming from the time discretization of the IPS

[
23
,
22
,
29
,
6
,
9
]
. Cubature type algorithms, a class of weak approximation algorithms, for (Stratonovich) MV-SDEs have been proposed in
[
19
,
26
,
49
]
. Lastly, we mention
[
1
]
and its references for numerical methods to approximate MV-SDEs directly fully avoiding the IPS approach.

Motivation, weak order schemes and the non-Markovian Euler scheme for SDEs.

The classical Euler scheme is an easy to implement and ubiquitous method for the numerical approximation of solutions to SDEs. In the classic overdamped Langevin context, i.e. if one sets
\nabla V=0
in (
1.3
), the Euler scheme attains a strong and weak rate
\mathcal{O}(h)
(where
h
is the time-discretization stepsize) in either finite or infinite time horizon
[
48
,
50
]
. Informally, under certain conditions the Euler scheme’s weak error at time
T=Mh
and stepsize
h>0
(
M\in\mathbb{N}
) can be expressed (see, Talay–Tubaro
[
55
,
48
]
) in the form

\displaystyle\textrm{Weak Error}^{\textrm{Euler}}(h;T)=C_{T}h+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})\qquad\textrm{where}\quad\lim_{T\to\infty}C_{T}=\textrm{Const}>0.

(1.4)

Setting
\nabla V
=0 in (
1.1
) and denoting by
X^{h}_{t_{m}}
the numerical approximation to
X_{t_{m}}
, the following variant of the Euler scheme was initially proposed in 2013 by
Leimkuhler and Matthews
in
[
42
]
and fully analyzed in the following year in
[
43
]
:

\displaystyle X^{h}_{t_{m+1}}=X^{h}_{t_{m}}-\nabla U(X^{h}_{t_{m}})h+\frac{\sigma}{2}(\Delta W_{m}+\Delta W_{m+1})\ \ \textrm{with}\ \ \Delta W_{m+1}=W_{t_{m+1}}-W_{t_{m}},

(1.5)

for any
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
t_{m}=mh
.
This scheme is called the
Leimkuhler–Matthews method
or the
non-Markovian Euler scheme

[
42
,
43
]
since
X^{h}_{t_{m+1}}
is computed using the current and past Brownian increments,
\Delta W_{m+1}
and
\Delta W_{m}
, respectively.

It is shown in
[
43
]
that (
1.4
) holds for
T<\infty
(with a different
C_{T}
), but as
T\to\infty
one has

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}C_{T}=0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\lim_{T\to\infty}\textrm{Weak Error}^{\textrm{non-Mark.~Euler}}(h;T)=\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

and thus the
non-Markovian Euler scheme
is a weak order-2 method as
T\to\infty
.
An intuition behind the result is offered by
[
56
]
through the concept of
Postprocessed integrators
. There, (
1.5
) is re-written as a two-step method where the second step corrects the
\mathcal{O}(h)
bias of the first step in such a way that in the long time limit the weak error is
\mathcal{O}(h^{2})
; see
[
56
, Equation (2.4)]
.

The focus of this work
is to study the non-Markovian Euler scheme (
1.5
) in the context of the overdamped MFL dynamics (
1.1
) as way to simulate (
1.2
) via a higher-order weak scheme.
As described, the MFL (
1.1
) is first approximated by the IPS (
1.3
) and then the IPS is time-discretized using the non-Markovian scheme (
1.5
).

In terms of proof methodologies for weak errors, for either SDEs or MV-SDEs, it is well known since the seminal work
[
55
]
, that weak error analysis can be tackled via the Kolmogorov backward PDE
[
48
, Chap. 2]
. This approach for MV-SDEs and the IPS is well-reviewed in
[
35
]
and is the approach we take. An alternative method is the use of Malliavin Calculus
[
4
,
38
,
49
]
as it offers a path to completely bypass the analysis of the Kolmogorov backward equation. In addition, we highlight the classic backward error analysis approach drawing on Itô- or Stratonovich–Taylor expansions
[
37
,
48
,
32
]
. In a different spirit, results showing density approximations, via Fokker–Plank PDE analysis or Malliavin calculus have been obtained in
[
11
,
10
,
3
,
5
]
.

The scheme’s convergence results for the MFL class.

The main contribution of this paper is to establish the techniques needed to understand and quantify the weak errors for the non-Markovian Euler scheme applied to (
1.3
)
in a way such that the convergence rate is independent of the number of particles
N
in the IPS
.
In terms of the convergence results, for any
T>0
, the weak approximation error for smooth test functions
g:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
(satisfying Assumption
2
) is defined as

\displaystyle\textrm{Weak Error}:=\mathbb{E}\big[g\big(\bm{X}_{T}^{N}\big)\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[g\big(\bm{X}^{N,h}_{T}\big)\big],\qquad\textrm{ with $T=Mh$ for $h>0,M\in\mathbb{N}$},

(1.6)

where
\bm{X}_{T}^{N}
denotes the solution of (
1.3
) and
\bm{X}^{N,h}_{T}
denotes the
\mathbb{R}^{N}
-valued output of
M
-steps (
T=Mh
) of the non-Markovian Euler scheme applied to (
1.3
) (and explicitly given in (
2.6
)).
Informally, our main result (Theorem
3.3
) states that

\displaystyle\big|\,\mathbb{E}[g(\bm{X}^{N}_{T})]-\mathbb{E}[g(\bm{X}^{N,h}_{T})]\,\big|

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-\lambda_{0}T)Kh+Kh^{3/2},

(1.7)

for some positive constants
\lambda_{0},K
independent of
h,T,M
and
N
. In other words, the scheme is uniformly (in the number of particles) of weak order
\mathcal{O}(h^{3/2})
as
T\to\infty
and has standard weak order
\mathcal{O}(h)
for
T<\infty
.
We provide an in-depth technical discussion (Remarks
3.1
and
6.1
) on the missing
h^{1/2}
order in the convergence rate when comparing this to the second order weak convergence result obtained in
[
43
,
56
]
.

A secondary contribution of this work (Proposition
2.2
below), is the clarification of the nuance that the higher-order weak convergence of the non-Markovian Euler scheme comes at the cost of having a
uniform in time
strong
L^{2}
-convergence order of
\mathcal{O}(h^{1/2})
. It is lower than the
\mathcal{O}(h)
strong
L^{2}
-convergence of the classical Euler scheme.

Methodology, contributions and existing literature.

The main methodology we follow is an involved variant of the Talay–Tubaro approach to the study of weak convergence
[
55
,
48
]
, which is also the approach used by
[
43
]
(for SDEs) and
[
35
,
18
]
(to study weak quantitative PoC over
T<\infty
). At its core, this method relates the expectations appearing in the definition of the weak error (
1.6
) to a Kolmogorov backward PDE with terminal condition given by the function
g
– see the PDE (
3.4
) linked to the driving SDE (
1.3
), which in flow form is given in (
3.3
) and written as
(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{s})_{s\geq t\geq 0}
, for
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
denoting the starting point of the IPS at time
t\geq 0
.
This analysis involves establishing certain bounds for the variation processes of the IPS (
1.3
), or more precisely for the flow process
(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{s})_{s\geq t\geq 0}
.
In this regard, our approach is closest in spirit to that of
[
35
]
as we work with Kolmogorov backward PDEs connected to the full particle system. However, our focus is on the
time-discretization
analysis
uniformly in
N
over infinite time
as opposed to the weak error analysis in the number of particles
[
35
,
28
,
18
]
(these works consider
T<\infty
and deal only with the continuous time IPS equation). Our case has therefore fundamental added complexities in relation to the mentioned works, as we require estimates which are not only uniform in
N
but also in time.

Technical challenges.

As mentioned, (
1.7
) is proved via a Talay–Tubaro type expansion which, for the case of the non-Markovian Euler scheme, is an involved collection of terms arising from Taylor expansions using the Kolmogorov backward equation associated with the flow equation for the IPS (
1.3
). This expansion has been given in
[
43
, Equation (3.17)]
for SDEs and we recast it to our setting (in Lemma
3.2
and also in Section
6
and in Appendix
A.5
). In the following, we highlight several technical elements of our work and point out crucial differences to
[
43
,
35
]
:

(i)

The terms arising from said Taylor expansions involve up to
6
-th order (cross)-derivatives in the spatial variable of the solution to the Kolmogorov backward PDE (see our Assumption
2
and Lemma
5.2
).
Critically, the usual pointwise estimates from PDE theory e.g.
[
43
, Equation (3.3)]
(or
[
54
,
56
]
), do not directly apply to our case as those would not be independent of the number of particles. It is not clear how the right-hand side in
[
43
, Equality (3.3)]
depends on the problem’s dimension.
Therefore, we derive suitable new estimates in
L^{p}
-norm of the solution to the Kolmogorov backward equation that decay exponentially in time in a non-explosive way in
N
(see, Lemma
5.2
for an intermediate pointwise result and Lemma
5.3
for the final
L^{p}
-estimates used to show the main theorem) – this is in stark contrast to
[
35
]
(in particular their Appendix B) which establishes pointwise estimates.
For clarity, the derivatives of the solution to the Kolmogorov backward equation are intrinsically linked to certain moment estimates for variation processes of the IPS’ flow SDE
(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{s})_{s\geq t}
(see Lemma
5.2
). In order to control the time dependence of the implied constants for the moment estimates of the variation processes, a careful analysis of the terms involving the convolution kernel is needed. Consequently, we are only able to establish the bounds in Lemma
5.3
in an
L^{p}
-sense. The estimates of Lemma
5.3
are obtained in
[
35
]
in a pointwise sense but crucially without the exponential time decay component (see RHS of (
5.16
) and (
5.17
)); their analysis is carried out in finite time for which this issue is not a concern.

Further, our analysis requires to study the time regularity of the solution to the Kolmogorov backward PDE, which needs estimates for the differences of the IPS’ flow SDE process,
(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{s})_{s\geq t}
, concretely differences of the form
|\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{u}-\bm{X}^{s,\bm{x},N}_{u}|
for
0\leq t\leq s\leq u
.

Lastly, it is noteworthy to highlight that the weak error test function
g
in (
1.6
) depends on the whole IPS (as in
[
35
]
but not as in
[
7
]
1
1

1

The analysis of the Kolmogorov backward PDE over a single-particle
X^{i,N}
instead of
\bm{X}^{N}=(X^{1,N},\ldots,X^{N,N})
on the test function
g
enables an advantageous simplifying decoupling effect at a later point; such is not the case here.
) making the analysis much more involved.

(ii)

Before addressing the estimates for the Kolmogorov backward equation, we derive
L^{p}
-norm estimates for the
variation processes
of the flow of the IPS (decaying over time uniformly over
N
) up to general
n
-order (although only
6
-orders are needed). This is done in Section
4
.
Our approach shows a way to analyze the terms arising from the interacting kernel and their recurring contributions across the different orders of the variation processes and
across different particle indices
– compare (
4.11
) and (
4.12
)
for the first order case and check Lemma
4.5
for general cases. A further crucial component of the analysis is to establish the correct decay in terms of number of particles across different orders of variation processes. This, in particular, subsequently allows to control the growth of the derivatives of the solution to the Kolmogorov backward equation.
The depth of the analysis is well beyond the results of
[
35
]
who carry out a related approach over finite time (or in
[
28
]
over the torus over infinite time horizon).

(iii)

Regarding the strong convergence analysis of the non-Markovian Euler scheme, the one-timestep error propagation analysis requires analysing 3 sub-steps of the scheme which is in contrast to such analysis for the standard Euler scheme (loosely speaking, only 1 sub-step is analyzed) and thus, the analysis is lengthier than usual – see e.g. the proof of Proposition
2.2
.

Gaps, conjectures and pathways for further study.

Our analysis addresses MFL dynamics in
\mathbb{R}
through
\mathbb{R}^{N}
-valued IPS. It is believed that our results could be established in the multi-dimensional case
d>1
if the measure dependence in (
1.1
) was of the form
\mathbb{E}[h(X_{t})]
instead of an interaction kernel.
The tools and techniques we have employed to show our main result do not use Lions measure derivatives (due to the simplicity of the underlying model) or concentration inequalities. It seems possible, although presently unclear, that drawing on Log-Sobolev inequalities and related machinery would provide means to lift the technical constraint in dimension arising from the convolution term.
Overall, to establish higher
L^{p}
-moments for the variation processes in Section
4
, we require the
symmetrization trick
(in Remark
4.1
to deal with (
4.5
)) and
an inequality of the type
(|x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y)\cdot\left(x-y\right)\geq 0
to hold – this would not naturally hold in the
d>1
case (this issued is hinted at in
[
23
]
and appears explicitly in
[
22
]
).
A possible alternative methodology to establish our main result is the postprocessed integrators machinery presented in
[
56
]
. To use it, we benefit from all the results shown in this work. However, some others would still need to be established, e.g. one has to derive results that imply Assumption 2.3 or Theorem 4.1 of
[
56
]
in the IPS (
1.3
) setting that remain uniform in the particle number; this is yet to be explored and left for future research.
In addition, it would be interesting to see if techniques from Malliavin calculus could be used
[
4
,
38
,
49
]
, even in the standard SDE context, to establish the weak convergence results shown in this article. It is interesting to question if the weak results in our manuscript could be extended to the difference between the densities of (
1.1
) and (
1.3
) as in
[
5
, Corollary 2.1]
(as the diffusion of (
1.3
) is uniformly elliptic) – in fact, the question is also pertinent in the context of standard SDEs itself (do the results of
[
43
,
56
]
also hold for densities as in
[
5
]
).

Further afield and more broadly is if these results could be established under the setting of common-noise MFL dynamics
[
44
]
, or in the context of the kinetic/underdamped MFL
[
34
,
20
,
8
]
.
Our work also paves the way to study stochastic gradient descent convergence
[
52
,
39
]
but using the non-Markovian Euler scheme as the update instead of the standard Euler one.

Paper organization.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we state the main assumptions, introduce the non-Markovian scheme and state basic results regarding wellposedness of the underlying model. In Section
3
, we present our weak error expansions based on the result in
[
43
]
. We state the main technical difficulties when applying the scheme to the IPS and explain why we cannot reach weak rate of order
2
in the case for classical SDEs. All proofs of Section
2
and
3
are postponed to the final part of the paper. Section
4
contains the results relating to the analysis of several variation processes, while Section
5
contains the decay estimates for the solution to the Kolmogorov backward equation. Section
6
contains the proof of the weak error result (of Section
3
).
An illustrative numerical example is provided in Section
7
.

Acknowledgments.

The authors are grateful to (in no particular order): B. Leimkuhler (U. of Edinburgh), A. Teckentrup (U. of Edinburgh), D. Higham (U. of Edinburgh), M. Tretyakov (U. of Nottingham), F. Delarue (U. Côte d’Azur) and A.-L. Haji-Ali (Heriot-Watt U.) for the helpful discussions.

2
Framework and numerical scheme

2.1
Notation and spaces

For a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
d\geq 1
, we will write
\bm{x}\coloneqq(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The inner product of two vectors
\bm{a},\bm{b}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
\left\langle\bm{a},\bm{b}\right\rangle
and for standard Euclidean norm we will use the notation
|\cdot|
. Throughout this article
\mathcal{O}(\cdot)
refers to the standard Landau (big ‘O’)
notation.
For a twice continuously differentiable function
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
\partial_{x_{i}}f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
the partial derivative with respect to the
i
-th component, by
\nabla f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
its gradient
\nabla f=(\partial_{x_{1}}f,\ldots,\partial_{x_{d}}f)
,
and by
\nabla^{2}f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
its Hessian.
For a multi-index
\ell=(\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
, we denote higher-order derivatives as

\displaystyle\partial^{d}_{x_{\ell_{1}},\ldots,x_{\ell_{d}}}f.

The sup-norm of
f
will be denoted by

|f|_{\infty}\coloneqq\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|f(\bm{x})|
.

Let our probability space be a completion of
(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with
\mathbb{F}=\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
being the natural filtration of the one-dimensional Brownian motion
W=(W_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, augmented with a sufficiently rich sub
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}_{0}
independent of
W
. We denote by
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]=\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}}[\cdot]
, the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}
.

For any
p\geq 2
, we define
L^{p}(\Omega,\mathbb{R}^{d})
as the space of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued measurable random variables such that
\mathbb{E}[\,|X|^{p}]^{\frac{1}{p}}<\infty
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the space of probability measures
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{p}\mu(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
. Let

\displaystyle W^{(p)}(\mu,\nu):=\inf_{\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)}\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{p}\pi(\mathrm{d}x,\mathrm{d}y)\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}},\quad\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

be the Wasserstein distance, where
\Pi(\mu,\nu)
is the set of couplings for
\mu
and
\nu
such that
\pi\in\Pi(\mu,\nu)
is a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\pi(\cdot\times\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mu
and
\pi(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\cdot)=\nu
.

2.2
Theoretical framework and preliminary results

We consider the following one-dimensional MV-SDE, for
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle X_{t}=\xi-\int_{0}^{t}\Big(\nabla U(X_{s})+\nabla V*\mu_{s}(X_{s})\Big)\mathrm{d}s+\sigma W_{t},

(2.1)

where
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
,
and
\xi\in L^{p}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
for some given
p\geq 2
.
U:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is the confining potential and
V:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is the interaction potential, with
*
denoting the usual convolution operator where
(f*\mu)(\cdot)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\cdot-y)\mu(\mathrm{d}y)
. We impose the following standard assumptions on
U
and
V
.

Assumption H.​​ 1
.

Let

U:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
V:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be twice continuously differentiable functions with globally Lipschitz continuous gradients. Further suppose that

1.

U
is uniformly convex in the sense that there exists
\lambda>0
such that for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\big(\nabla U(x)-\nabla U(y)\big)\big(x-y\big)\geq\lambda|x-y|^{2},

(2.2)

which implies
\nabla^{2}U\geq\lambda
.

2.

V
is even (thus
\nabla V
is odd), and convex, i.e., for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\big(\nabla V(x)-\nabla V(y)\big)(x-y)\geq 0,

and there exists
K_{V}>0
such that
|\nabla^{2}V|_{\infty}\leq K_{V}
.

The Interacting particle system (IPS).

Define the
\mathbb{R}^{N}
-valued map
B
as

\mathbb{R}^{N}\ni\bm{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\mapsto B(\bm{x}):=\big(B_{1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}),\ldots,B_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\big),

where

\displaystyle B_{i}(\bm{x})=B_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\coloneqq-\nabla U(x_{i})-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\nabla V(x_{i}-x_{j}).

(2.3)

Let
(\xi^{i},W^{i})
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
be i.i.d. copies of
(\xi,W)
and define the IPS associated with (
2.1
) to be

\displaystyle X_{t}^{i,N}

\displaystyle=\xi^{i}+\int_{0}^{t}B_{i}(X^{1,N}_{s},\ldots,X^{N,N}_{s})\mathrm{d}s+\sigma W^{i}_{t},

(2.4)

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{t}^{N}

\displaystyle=\bm{\xi}+\int_{0}^{t}B(\bm{X}_{s}^{N})\mathrm{d}s+\sigma\bm{W}_{t},

(2.5)

with solution process
(\bm{X}^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\coloneqq(X_{t}^{1,N},\ldots,X_{t}^{N,N})_{t\geq 0}
, where we introduced
\bm{\xi}=(\xi^{1},\ldots,\xi^{N})
and
(\bm{W}_{t})_{t\geq 0}:=(W^{1}_{t},\ldots,W^{N}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
.

Preliminary results.

The next proposition collects some basic properties of the MFL equation (
2.1
) and the IPS (
2.4
).

Proposition 2.1
.

Let Assumption
1
hold and let
\xi\in L^{p}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
for some
p\geq 2
.
Then the following hold:

1.

The MV-SDE (
2.1
) and the IPS (
2.4
) each admit a unique strong solution.
There exist constants
\kappa\in(0,\lambda)
and
K\geq 0
(
K,\kappa
are independent of
t
and
N
) such that for any
t\geq 0

\mathbb{E}\big[\,|X_{t}|^{p}\big]\leq K\big(1+\mathbb{E}[\,|\xi|^{p}]e^{-p\kappa t}\big)\quad\textrm{and}\quad\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}\mathbb{E}\big[\,|X_{t}^{i,N}|^{p}\big]\leq K\big(1+\mathbb{E}[\,|\xi|^{p}]e^{-p\kappa t}\big).

2.

Uniform in time strong propagation of chaos (PoC) holds, i.e., there exist constants
\kappa\in(0,\lambda)
and
K\geq 0
(
K,\kappa
are independent of
t
and
N
) such that for every
N\geq 1

\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}\sup_{t\geq 0}\mathbb{E}\big[\,|X^{i}_{t}-X_{t}^{i,N}|^{2}\big]\leq\frac{K}{N}\Big(1+\mathbb{E}[\,|\xi|^{2}]e^{-2\kappa t}\Big),

where
X^{i}
is the solution of (
2.1
) with
(\xi,W)
replaced by
(\xi^{i},W^{i})
(i.e., the so-called non-interacting particle system).

3.

There exists a unique stationary distribution for (
2.1
) and (
2.4
), denoted by
\mu^{*}
and
\mu^{N,*}
, respectively. Moreover,
W^{(2)}(\mu_{t},\mu^{*})\to 0
and
W^{(2)}(\mu_{t}^{N},\mu^{N,*})\to 0
as
t\to\infty
.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.1
.
∎

2.3
The non-Markovian Euler scheme

Let
h\in(0,1)
denote the timestep and take
m\in\{0,\ldots,M-1\}
for a given
M\in\mathbb{N}
. Inspired by
[
43
, Equation (1.7)]
(also
[
56
]
), we introduce the following non-Markovian Euler scheme

\displaystyle X^{i,N,h}_{t_{m+1}}=X^{i,N,h}_{t_{m}}-\Big(\nabla U(X^{i,N,h}_{t_{m}})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\nabla V(X_{t_{m}}^{i,N,h}-X_{t_{m}}^{j,N,h})\Big)h+\frac{\sigma}{2}(\Delta W_{m}^{i}+\Delta W_{m+1}^{i}),

(2.6)

with
X^{i,N,h}_{t_{0}}=X^{i,N}_{t_{0}}
, to approximate the IPS (
2.4
), where we set the time grid points as
t_{m}:=mh
up to some time
T:=t_{M}=Mh
, and the random increments as
\Delta W_{m+1}^{i}=W^{i}_{t_{m+1}}-W^{i}_{t_{m}}
with
\Delta W^{i}_{0}=0
.
In analogy to the IPS, we write for the solution process

(\bm{X}^{N,h}_{t_{m}})_{m\in\{0,\ldots,M\}}\coloneqq(X^{1,N,h}_{t_{m}},\ldots,X^{N,N,h}_{t_{m}})_{m\in\{0,\ldots,M\}}
. We aim to analyze the behaviour of this scheme as
T\to\infty
.

The following result establishes some fundamental properties for the non-Markovian Euler scheme (
2.6
): moment estimates and
L^{2}
-strong convergence (we were unable to find a proof in the literature regarding the
L^{2}
-strong convergence for this scheme (even for SDEs) and we thus provide it here). Critically, the moment estimates obtained are independent of the time horizon (i.e., the constant
K
appearing below is independent of
T
). Lastly, as in
[
43
]
or
[
56
]
, the result holds for a sufficiently small timestep
h
.

Proposition 2.2
.

Let Assumption
1
hold, let
\xi\in L^{p}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
for some
p\geq 2
, let
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
,
h>0
and set
T=t_{M}=Mh
.
Then the following statements hold for the process defined in (
2.6
).

1.

There exist
\kappa,K>0
(both are independent of
h,T,M
and
N
) such that for any sufficiently small timestep
h
(chosen independently of
T,M,N
) and satisfying
0<h<\min\{1/2\lambda,1\}
it holds for all
m\in\{0,\ldots,M\}
that

\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}\mathbb{E}\big[\,|X_{t_{m}}^{i,N,h}|^{p}\big]\leq K\big(1+\mathbb{E}\big[\,|\xi|^{p}\big]e^{-\kappa t_{m}}\big).

2.

L^{2}
-strong error.
There exists
K>0
(independent of
h,T,M
and
N
) such that for any sufficiently small timestep
h
(chosen independently of
T,M,N
) and satisfying
0<h<\min\{1/2\lambda,1\}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}\max_{m\in\{0,\ldots,M\}}\mathbb{E}\big[\,|X_{t_{m}}^{i,N}-X_{t_{m}}^{i,N,h}|^{2}\big]\leq Kh\big(1+\mathbb{E}\big[\,|\xi|^{2}\big]\big),

where
X^{i,N}
and
X^{i,N,h}
are the processes defined in (
2.4
) and (
2.6
) respectively.

Proof.

See Appendix
A.2
.
∎

3
The weak error expansion

Before presenting the framework for the weak error analysis and our main result, we require the following definition which will be helpful to characterize and analyze the higher-order variation processes.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
n,m,N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N\gg n,m
be given integers.
Define the set of multi-indices

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}^{N}:=\big\{\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}):\gamma_{i}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}~\textit{for all}~i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\big\},

with
\Pi_{0}^{N}\coloneqq\emptyset
denoting the empty set. For a subset
\overline{\gamma}\subseteq\gamma
, let
|\overline{\gamma}|
be its length.

For a given
\gamma\in\Pi_{n}^{N}
, let

\hat{\gamma}
be a set of length
N
counting the frequency of each
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
in
\gamma
, and define

\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma):=\{\text{number of non-zero values in}~\hat{\gamma}\}
.
We also use the following three operations for the multi-indices: for
\gamma^{(1)}\in\Pi_{n}^{N}
and
\gamma^{(2)}\in\Pi_{m}^{N}
, the difference
\gamma^{(1)}\setminus\gamma^{(2)}\in\Pi_{k}^{N}
is specified through the counting set (as
|\hat{\gamma}^{(1)}|=|\hat{\gamma}^{(2)}|=N
)

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}^{\textrm{diff}}\coloneqq\{\max\{\hat{\gamma}^{(1)}_{1}-\hat{\gamma}^{(2)}_{1},0\},\ldots,\max\{\hat{\gamma}^{(1)}_{N}-\hat{\gamma}^{(2)}_{N},0\}\},

where
k
is the number of non-zero elements in
2
2

2

In this work the difference
\gamma^{(1)}\setminus\gamma^{(2)}
( also, the intersection
\gamma^{(1)}\bigcap\gamma^{(2)}
) is
never
used directly, only the quantity
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma^{(1)}\setminus\gamma^{(2)})
(also,
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma^{(1)}\bigcap\gamma^{(2)})
) will be used and thus we require only
\hat{\gamma}^{\textrm{diff}}
(also,
\hat{\gamma}^{\textrm{intersec}}
). For practical purposes, one can think of the
\gamma
as being ordered vectors (in increasing order) – see Example
3.1
.

\hat{\gamma}^{\textrm{diff}}
.

Similarly, the intersection
\gamma^{(1)}\bigcap\gamma^{(2)}\in\Pi_{k}^{N}
is specified through the counting set

\displaystyle\hat{\gamma}^{\textrm{intersec}}\coloneqq\{\min\{\hat{\gamma}^{(1)}_{1},\hat{\gamma}^{(2)}_{1}\},\ldots,\min\{\hat{\gamma}^{(1)}_{N},\hat{\gamma}^{(2)}_{N}\}\},

where
k
is the number of non-zero elements.

The union is defined by

\displaystyle\gamma^{(1)}\bigcup\gamma^{(2)}=(\gamma^{(1)}_{1},\dots,\gamma^{(1)}_{n},\gamma^{(2)}_{1},\dots,\gamma^{(2)}_{m})\in\Pi_{n+m}^{N}.

For two sets of multi-indices,
\Pi_{n}^{N}
and
\Pi_{m}^{N}
, with
n\neq m
, the union is defined as

\displaystyle\Pi_{n}^{N}\bigcup\Pi_{m}^{N}=\{\gamma:\gamma\in\Pi_{n}^{N}\text{ or }\gamma\in\Pi_{m}^{N}\}.

The shuffle product
3
3

3

The shuffle product
\shuffle
is the binary operation that takes two ordered sequences (multi-indices for our purposes) and creates all possible ways of interleaving them while preserving the relative order of elements in each sequence.
In a nutshell, the shuffle operation ensures that the relative order of elements within each original sequence is preserved and all possible interleavings are included – see Example
3.1
.

(see,
[
31
]
) for two multi-indices
\gamma^{(1)}\in\Pi_{n}^{N}
and
\gamma^{(2)}\in\Pi_{m}^{N}
is denoted by
\gamma^{(1)}\shuffle\gamma^{(2)}
. We write
\gamma^{(1)}\simeq\gamma^{(2)}
if
m=n
and there exists a permutation
\pi\in S_{n}
such that
(\gamma^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{(1)}_{n})=(\gamma^{(2)}_{\pi(1)},\ldots,\gamma^{(2)}_{\pi(n)})
, where
S_{n}
is the symmetric group on the set
\{1,\ldots,N\}
.

Example 3.1
.

We present the following examples to make Definition
3.1
clearer: For
N=3
, we have:

\displaystyle\Pi_{1}^{3}=\big\{(1

\displaystyle),(2),(3)\big\},\quad\Pi_{2}^{3}=\big\{(1,1),(1,2),(1,3),(2,1),(2,2),(2,3),(3,1),(3,2),(3,3)\big\}.

For
N\geq 3
, let
(1,1,1)
(i.e.,
\hat{\gamma}=(3,0,\dots,0),~|\hat{\gamma}|=N
),
(1,1,2)
(i.e.,
\hat{\gamma}=(2,1,0,\dots,0)
) ,
(1,2,2)\in\Pi_{3}^{N}
(i.e.,
\hat{\gamma}=(1,2,0,\dots,0)
). Then we have

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{O}}\big((1,1,1)\big)=1,\quad\hat{\mathcal{O}}\big((1,1,2)\big)=2,\quad\hat{\mathcal{O}}\big((1,2,3)\big)=3.

With regards to the set operations, we offer an example for
\alpha=(1,2,3,3),\beta=(3,5)
:

\displaystyle\alpha\setminus\beta=(1,2,3),\quad\alpha\bigcup\beta=(1,2,3,3,3,5).

In regards to the shuffle product, take for example
\alpha=(1,2)
,
\beta=(3)
, then

\displaystyle\alpha\shuffle\beta

\displaystyle=\big\{(3,1,2),(1,3,2),(1,2,3)\big\}.

It should be apparent that
\hat{\mathcal{O}}
yields the same result for all elements in the shuffle product
\alpha\shuffle\beta
, and we can understand
(1,2,3)\simeq(1,3,2)\simeq(2,1,3)\simeq(2,3,1)\simeq(3,1,2)\simeq(3,2,1)
.

Lastly, the following summation of the elements in an
N\times N
matrix with elements
a_{i,j}
for
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, demonstrates the meaning of
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
in the definition for the case
n=2
:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}a_{i,j}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Pi_{2}^{N}}a_{{\gamma_{1}},{\gamma_{2}}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\Pi_{2}^{N},\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma)=1\end{subarray}}a_{{\gamma_{1}},{\gamma_{2}}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma\in\Pi_{2}^{N},\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma)=2\end{subarray}}a_{{\gamma_{1}},{\gamma_{2}}},

where we partitioned the summation into diagonal and off-diagonal elements.

For our analysis, we impose the following assumption.

Assumption H.​​ 2
.

Assumption
1
holds. Further, suppose that:

1.

The potentials
U,V\in\mathcal{C}^{7}(\mathbb{R})
,
and all derivatives of
\nabla U,\nabla V
are uniformly bounded. (This in particular implies that
\nabla U,\nabla V
are Lipschitz continuous.)

2.

The convexity parameters
\lambda,K_{V}
satisfy
\lambda\geq 7K_{V}
.

3.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
with
N\gg 6
.
For any
n\in\{1,\ldots,6\}
and
(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{|\gamma|})=\gamma\in\bigcup_{k=1}^{n}\Pi_{k}^{N}
, with integers
\gamma_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,
there exists a constant
K
independent of
N
such that
the function
g:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, satisfies

|\partial^{|\gamma|}_{{x_{\gamma_{1}},\dots,x_{\gamma_{|\gamma|}}}}g|_{\infty}\leq K\cdot N^{-\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma)}
.

4.

The function
g
and its derivatives up to order
n
are Lipschitz. (Note that item (3) implies that the function
g
and its derivatives up to order
n-1
are Lipschitz.)

Remark 3.1
.

(On point (1) of Assumption
2
):
Our analysis has a small reduction regarding the order of regularity when compared to
[
43
]
who require the drift of the underlying model to be 8-times continuously differentiable. In
[
43
]
, in an intermediate step, weak convergence of order
\mathcal{O}(h)
is first established for the non-Markovian Euler scheme (which only requires the drift to be 6-times continuously differentiable).

The intermediate result is then employed to show weak convergence of a certain term where the test function involves 2nd order derivatives of the drift and the solution to the Kolmogorov PDE. In our notation this test function is denoted by
L
and is precisely defined in (
3.7
) below.
Since
L
already involves second order derivatives (of the potentials), the higher regularity is needed. We are not able to show that
L
possesses sufficient regularity properties (i.e., item (3) in Assumption
2
) to apply the intermediate result concerning the first order weak convergence and resort to a strong convergence result instead (as a consequence we only derive the weak convergence rate 1.5 instead of 2); see Remark
6.1
for full details.

Remark 3.2

(On point (2) of Assumption
2
)
.

The constraint
\lambda\geq 7K_{V}
is not sharp and relates to the need of sufficient convexity as we analyze the
n
-th order variation processes (for the process defined in (
3.3
)).
For instance, Lemma
4.4
establishes moment bounds of the
2
nd variation processes of (
3.3
) and for it we require
\lambda>(2+1/N)K_{V}
in (
4.56
). For the moment bounds of the
6
-th variation processes (in Lemma
4.5
), the requirement ends up being
\lambda>(6+1/N)K_{V}
and we streamline it to
\lambda\geq 7K_{V}
. This is a technical constraint of the analysis stemming from the interplay between the confinement potential and the interaction kernel functions and will be made more precise in the proofs of the lemmas. Lastly, this assumption is not comparable to those in
[
43
]
or
[
56
]
as neither have interaction kernels; only confining potentials (see (
2.1
)).

Remark 3.3

(On point (3) of Assumption
2
)
.

Typical examples for
g
satisfying the above assumptions would be
g(\bm{x})=\tilde{g}\left(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}f(x_{i})\right)
, for some functions
f,\tilde{g}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
that are sufficiently often differentiable with bounded derivatives. We provide a few examples.

•

For instance, consider the case
n=3

and let
\gamma\in\Pi_{1}^{N}\bigcup\Pi_{2}^{N}\bigcup\Pi_{3}^{N}
for which
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma)=3
(e.g.,

\gamma=(1,2,3)
). Therefore, our assumption requires
|\partial^{3}_{x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}}{g}|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-3})
, which is satisfied for regular enough functions
\tilde{g}
and
f
.

As a further example, consider
\gamma=(1,1,3)
for which
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma)=2
, and hence,
|\partial^{3}_{x_{1},x_{1},x_{3}}{g}|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-2})
.

•

If
f=\textrm{id}
then for any
|\gamma|
-order derivative, one has
|\partial^{|\gamma|}_{{x_{\gamma_{1}},\dots,x_{\gamma_{|\gamma|}}}}{g}|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-|\gamma|})
.

•

Let
\gamma
be
k
-dimensional with
k\geq 2
and set
\tilde{g}=\textrm{id}
.

Then, any cross derivative of
g
satisfies
\partial^{2}_{x_{i}x_{j}}g(\bm{x})=0
for
i\neq j
and only when
i=j
we have
\partial^{2}_{x_{i}x_{i}}g(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{N}f^{\prime\prime}(x_{i})
. Thus
|\partial^{|\gamma|}_{{x_{\gamma_{1}},\dots,x_{\gamma_{|\gamma|}}}}{g}|_{\infty}=0
whenever
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma)\geq 2
and
|\partial^{|\gamma|}_{{x_{\gamma_{1}},\dots,x_{\gamma_{|\gamma|}}}}{g}|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
whenever
\hat{\mathcal{O}}(\gamma)=1
.

•

These observations hold if adapted to the multi-dimensional case at the cost of an increased notational complexity. Without offering too many details, assume the particle system is set in
\bm{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
where
x_{i}=(x_{i,1},\ldots,x_{i,d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and let
g:(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
,
\tilde{g}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
. Let
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
.

A few easy calculations show immediately, using the
\nabla
gradient notation (as maps are multidimensional), that

|\nabla_{x_{i}}g(\bm{x})|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})\quad\textrm{and}\quad|\nabla_{x_{j}}\big(\nabla_{x_{i}}g(\bm{x})\big)|_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})\mathbbm{1}_{i=j}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-2}).

Lastly, for the reader familiar with the language of Lions derivatives and flows of measures, it is possible to find (sufficiency) conditions on the derivatives of the measure functional
G:\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
so that
g(\bm{x}):=G\big(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}\big)
satisfies the Assumption 3.3 (3). For the case
n=2
(with
\gamma\in\Pi_{1}^{N}\bigcup\Pi_{2}^{N}
), this is easily seen using the idea of empirical projection map and
[
14
, Proposition 5.91 and Proposition 5.35]
. In essence, for the case
n=2
, a (strong) sufficient condition would be for the derivatives
\partial_{v}\partial_{\mu}G
and
\partial^{2}_{\mu\mu}G
to exist and be uniformly bounded.

Weak discretization error.

We define the weak discretization error induced by the non-Markovian scheme (see, (
2.6
)) approximating the IPS
\bm{X}_{T}^{N}
(
2.4
) as follows: Let the test function
g:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy Assumption
2
. For any
T>0
, the weak approximation error is the worst case (over
g
) of

\displaystyle\textrm{Weak (discretization) Error}:=\mathbb{E}\big[g\big(\bm{X}_{T}^{N}\big)\big]-\mathbb{E}\big[g\big(\bm{X}^{N,h}_{T}\big)\big].

(3.1)

The Kolmogorov backward equation for the flow and the weak error expansion.

We study the weak error (
3.1
) via an analysis of the Kolmogorov backward equation for the stochastic flow equation associated with the dynamics of (
2.5
). Concretely, let
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and
0\leq t\leq s
. Then we introduce
\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{s}=(X^{t,x_{1},1,N}_{s},\ldots,X^{t,x_{N},N,N}_{s})
, where

\displaystyle X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s}

\displaystyle=x_{i}+\int_{t}^{s}B_{i}(X^{t,x_{1},1,N}_{u},\ldots,X^{t,x_{N},N,N}_{u})\mathrm{d}u+\sigma(W^{i}_{s}-W^{i}_{t}),\quad i\in\{1,\ldots,N\},

(3.2)

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{s}^{t,\bm{x},N}

\displaystyle=\bm{x}+\int_{t}^{s}B(\bm{X}_{r}^{t,\bm{x},N})\mathrm{d}r+\sigma(\bm{W}_{s}-\bm{W}_{t}).

(3.3)

The wellposedness of (
3.2
) or (
3.3
) under our assumptions is clear. The generator for (
3.3
) is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{N}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}B_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}\partial^{2}_{x_{i},x_{i}},

where
B_{i}
is the drift term for the
i
-th particle as in (
3.2
).
Now, for
u:[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
, we introduce the Kolmogorov backward equation:

\partial_{t}u+\mathcal{L}_{N}u=0,\quad t\in[0,T),\quad u(T,\bm{x})=g(\bm{x}),

(3.4)

for the above test function
g:\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
.
Under the above assumptions, the solution of the above PDE is given by the Feynman-Kac formula
[
48
,
33
]
:

\displaystyle u(t,\bm{x})=\mathbb{E}\left[g(\bm{X}_{T}^{N})|X_{t}^{i,N}=x_{i},i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}\right].

(3.5)

To analyze the weak error (
3.1
), we need to expand it akin to a Talay–Tubaro expansion
[
55
]
(see also
[
48
,
35
]
), but with certain fundamental differences. The following expansion is shown in
[
43
]
.

Lemma 3.2

(Weak error expansion, Equation (3.17) in
[
43
]
)
.

Let Assumption
2
hold, then the following expansion of the weak error holds for the processes defined in (
2.4
) and (
2.6
).

Take any sufficiently small timestep
h
(chosen independently of
T,M,N
) and satisfying
0<h<\min\{1/2\lambda,1\}
. Set
m\in\{0,\ldots,M-1\}
for a given
M\in\mathbb{N}
(recall
T=t_{M}=Mh
), we then have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[g(\bm{X}^{N}_{T})]-\mathbb{E}[g(\bm{X}^{N,h}_{T})]

\displaystyle=h^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}L(t_{m},\bm{X}^{N,h}_{t_{m}})\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{m=0}^{M-1}R(t_{m},\bm{X}^{N,h}_{t_{m}})\right],

(3.6)

where
L:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{N}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined via the map
u
defined in (
3.5
) and the drifts
(B_{i})_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}
in (
2.4
) as

\displaystyle L(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Big[\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}B_{j}(\bm{x})\partial_{x_{j}}B_{i}(\bm{x})\partial_{x_{i}}u(t,\bm{x})+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\partial_{x_{j}}B_{i}(\bm{x})\partial^{2}_{x_{i},x_{j}}u(t,\bm{x})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\partial^{2}_{x_{j},x_{j}}B_{i}(\bm{x})\partial_{x_{i}}u(t,\bm{x})\Big],\quad t\geq 0,~\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},

(3.7)

and
R(\cdot,\cdot)
is a collection of remainder terms (discussed and analyzed in Section
6.2
).

Proof.

This expansion is derived and presented in
[
43
, Equation (3.17)]
and we do not reproduce it here. Our function
L
given in (
3.7
) is denoted as
B_{0}
in
[
43
]
(see their Theorem 3.4).
The sum of remainders
R(t_{m},\cdot)
in (
3.6
) corresponds to the second sum of remainders
h^{3}r(t_{m},\cdot)
in
[
43
, Equation (3.17)]
– where the
h^{3}r(t_{m},\cdot)
itself is a linear combination of remainders
h^{3}r_{j}
for
j\in\{1,\ldots,8\}
appearing in Equations (3.8), (3.10)–(3.16) in
[
43
, p7-9]
. This derivation is discussed in more depth in Section
6.2
.
∎

We aim to control the growth of
L
and the remainder
R
in terms of quantities that do not grow in
N
– this is a central technical difference to
[
43
]
. A key point in the growth analysis for
L
(and
R
) is the suitable control of moment bounds for the variation processes of
(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{s})_{s\geq t\geq 0}
, which will be discussed in the next section.
We end this section with this manuscript’s main result. Its proof is given in Section
6
after establishing a large collection of auxiliary results in Sections
4
and
5
, and the appendix.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let Assumption
2
hold, let
\xi\in L^{10}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
and take a timestep
h
(chosen independently of
T,M,N
) sufficiently small satisfying
0<h<\min\{1/2\lambda,1\}
.
Then, the following expansion for the weak error for the processes defined in (
2.4
) and (
2.6
) holds: for any
N,M\in\mathbb{N}
(with
T
defined as
T=Mh
),

\displaystyle\big|\mathbb{E}[g(\bm{X}^{N}_{T})]

\displaystyle-\mathbb{E}[g(\bm{X}^{N,h}_{T})]\big|\leq|C_{0}(T)|h+Kh^{3/2},

where

\displaystyle C_{0}(T)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}L(t,\bm{X}^{N}_{t})\mathrm{d}t\right],\quad\textrm{and}\quad|C_{0}(T)|\leq K\exp(-\lambda_{0}T)+Kh^{1/2},

for some positive constants
\lambda_{0},K
independent of
h,T,M
and
N
.

Proof.

This result follows as a consequence of Lemmas
6.1
and
6.2
in Section
6
.
∎

It is clear from the main statement that, as
T\to\infty
, the weak error is of order
{3/2}
uniformly in
N
, and at finite time
T<\infty
it is of order
1
uniformly in
N
. We flag that for standard SDEs (without concern for uniformity in
N
), both
[
43
,
56
]
obtain an error of order
h^{2}
(as
T\to\infty
). This gap in our result is of technical nature (appearing in Section
6
) and is detailed in Remark
6.1
. The assumption that
\xi\in L^{10}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
stems from the analysis of the remainder terms
R
appearing in the weak error expansion (
3.6
) (see Section
6.2
). The analysis of the
L
term only requires
\xi\in L^{4}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
(see Section
6.1
).

Before moving into proofs and auxiliary results, we flag that Theorem
3.3
controls the weak discretization error between the non-Markovian scheme (
2.6
) and the IPS’ solution (
2.5
). To control the weak error between the non-Markovian scheme (
2.6
) and the initial MV-SDE (
2.1
) one requires only a triangle inequality with the IPS’ solution (
2.5
) alongside a quantitative uniform in time weak propagation of chaos result controlling the error from (
2.5
) to (
2.1
). The latter is provided by
[
8
, Theorem 1.1]
stating that such error is of order
\mathcal{O}(1/N)
uniformly over time
T
. In the time limit
T\to\infty
, the non-Markovian scheme approximates the original MV-SDE (in weak error) as
\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})+\mathcal{O}(h^{3/2})
.

4
Analysis of Variation processes

The results for the variation processes established below are key to studying the uniform-in-
N
and uniform-in-time decay of the solution to the Kolmogorov backward equation. For completeness, we state the following lemma regarding wellposedness of the multiple variation process used throughout this section and drawing from classical SDE theory. The subsequent results of the section are devoted to establishing
L^{p}
-estimates of these processes that decay exponentially in time in a non-explosive way in
N
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let Assumption
2
hold, and let
\mathbb{N}\ni n\leq 6
and
T>0
. For any
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, and
T\geq s\geq t\geq 0
, let
\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{s}
be defined by (
3.2
). Then its first
n
-variation processes given by
(
4.1
),
(
4.35
), and (
4.57
) have unique solutions.

Proof.

For any fixed
N
and
T>0
, Assumption
2
implies Assumption (A) (p108), condition (5.12) and condition (5.15) (at any higher-order; see pages 120 and 122, respectively) in
[
33
]
. This suffices to ensure the wellposedness of the first (
4.1
), second (
4.35
) and higher-order (
4.57
) variation processes via Theorem 5.3 and Theorem 5.4 in
[
33
]

(see the comment after the proof of
[
33
, Theorem 5.5 (p123)]
regarding the extension of Theorem 5.4 to higher order derivatives). Note that the analysis in the later sections of this article is carried out for an arbitrary
T>0
, and
T\to\infty
is only considered in the final step; in particular, a wellposedness result for the variation processes in finite time suffices for our purposes.
∎

4.1
First Variation process

Here and below let
T>0
be an arbitrary terminal time and let
T\geq s\geq t\geq 0
,
N\in\mathbb{N}
. The first variation process of
(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{s})_{s\geq t\geq 0}
defined in (
3.2
), is given by

\displaystyle X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}=\delta_{i,j}+\int_{t}^{s}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\partial_{x_{l}}B_{i}(\bm{X}_{u}^{t,\bm{x},N})X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}\mathrm{d}u,

(4.1)

where
\delta_{i,j}
is the usual Kronecker symbol. The subindex
x_{j}
in
X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}
indicates the perturbation with respect to the
j
-th component of the initial data
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
of the flow process
(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{s})_{s\geq t\geq 0}
. Note that the processes
(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}})_{s\geq t\geq 0}
(for different indices
i,j
) are, in general, not identically distributed. However, if the starting positions
x_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, are all sampled from the same distribution, then the ‘diagonal’ elements of
(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}_{s})_{s\geq t\geq 0}
are identically distributed. (The same argument applies to the off-diagonal ones). This first lemma accounts for the different behaviours of
L^{p}
-moments for (
4.1
).

Lemma 4.2
.

Let Assumption
2
hold and let
p\geq 2
.
Consider the first variation process
(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}})_{i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}}
defined by (
4.1
) for
T\geq s\geq t\geq 0
. Then there exist constants
\lambda_{1}\in(0,\lambda)
and
K>0
(both are independent of
s,t,T
and
N
) such that for any
T\geq s\geq t\geq 0

\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]\leq Ke^{-{\lambda p}(s-t)}\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]\leq\frac{K}{N^{p-1}}e^{-\lambda_{1}p(s-t)}.

(4.2)

This lemma and the earlier Remark
3.3
highlight the main difficulty faced in this manuscript’s analysis. The above inequalities suggest that the term
i=j
delivers the
\mathcal{O}(1)
behaviour while all other (cross-derivative
i\neq j
) elements decay proportionally to the number of particles. Our analysis throughout this section is involved, as this behaviour needs to be tracked across higher-order variation processes.

Lastly, since (
4.1
) is a linear ODE with random coefficients (bounded in
\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}
) and initial condition
\delta_{i,j}
we are able to obtain all
L^{p}
-moments without imposing further constraints on the integrability of
\bm{X}^{t,\bm{x},N}
.

Remark 4.1

(The ‘symmetrization trick’)
.

We employ a recurring argument in this proof which we coin as the
symmetrization trick
. This trick exploits that
\nabla^{2}V
is an even function. That is, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
we have
\nabla^{2}V(x)=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla^{2}V(x)+\nabla^{2}V(-x))
.

Proof.

Note that in the following proof, the positive constant
K
is independent of
s,t,T,N
and may change line by line. The essence of this proof is the application of Itô’s formula followed by standard domination arguments.

Applying Itô’s formula yields for any
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}

\displaystyle e^{\lambda p(s-t)}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{i,j}+p\int_{t}^{s}e^{\lambda p(u-t)}\bigg(\mathbb{E}\Big[X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\Big(\sum_{l=1}^{N}\partial_{x_{l}}B_{i}(\bm{X}_{u}^{t,\bm{x},N})X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}\Big)|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}\Big]+\lambda\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]\bigg)\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=\delta_{i,j}-p\int_{t}^{s}e^{\lambda p(u-t)}\bigg(\mathbb{E}\Big[X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\Big(\nabla^{2}U(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u})X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\Big)|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}\Big]-\lambda\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]\bigg)\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\quad-p\int_{t}^{s}e^{\lambda p(u-t)}\mathbb{E}\Big[X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\Big(\sum_{l=1}^{N}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\partial_{x_{l}}\nabla V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{k},k,N}_{u})X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}\Big)|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}\Big]\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{i,j}-p\int_{t}^{s}e^{\lambda p(u-t)}\mathbb{E}\Big[X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\Big(\sum_{l=1}^{N}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\partial_{x_{l}}\nabla V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{k},k,N}_{u})X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}\Big)|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}\Big]\mathrm{d}u,

where we used (
2.2
) of Assumption
1
to obtain

\displaystyle-X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\Big(\nabla^{2}U(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u})X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\Big)\leq-\lambda|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{2}.

(4.3)

We further note that by the chain rule,

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{N}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}

\displaystyle\partial_{x_{l}}\nabla V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{k},k,N}_{u})X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}).

(4.4)

Hence taking summation over
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
in (
4.4
), we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\Big(\sum_{l=1}^{N}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\partial_{x_{l}}\nabla V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{k},k,N}_{u})X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}\Big)|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\Big(|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}})\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\Big(|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}})\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Big(|X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u})(X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}})\Big)\Big]

(4.5)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\Big(~\big(|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}-|X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\cdot\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}})\Big)\Big]\geq 0,

(4.6)

where in (
4.6
), we used the inequality
\left(|x|^{p-2}x-|y|^{p-2}y\right)\cdot\left(x-y\right)\geq 0
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
and the fact that
\nabla^{2}V(x)\geq 0
, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.
In accordance to Remark
4.1
, we used the
symmetrization trick
to derive (
4.5
). Hence, taking summation over
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we deduce that

\displaystyle e^{\lambda p(s-t)}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{i,j}=1

and consequently for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]\leq e^{-\lambda p(s-t)}.

(4.7)

To prove the claim of the second result in (
4.2
), we first derive for any
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,
\lambda_{1}\in(0,\lambda)
(using Itô’s formula and (
4.3
)),

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{1}p(s-t)}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]\leq p\int_{t}^{s}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda)e^{\lambda_{1}(u-t)}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt-e^{\lambda_{1}(u-t)}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\Big(\sum_{l=1}^{N}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\partial_{x_{l}}\nabla V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{k},k,N}_{u})X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}\Big)\Big]\mathrm{d}u.

For the last term above, we note that

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\Big(\sum_{l=1}^{N}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\partial_{x_{l}}\nabla V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{k},k,N}_{u})X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\sum_{l=1,l\neq j}^{N}\Big(|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}\cdot\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}})\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\Big(|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}\cdot X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}},\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{u})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{u,x_{j}})\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq-\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\sum_{l=1,l\neq j}^{N}\Big(~\big(|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}-|X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-2}X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}}\big)\cdot\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u})

\displaystyle\quad\cdot(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{u,x_{j}})\Big)\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}|\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u}-X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{u})|~|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-1}~|X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{u,x_{j}}|\Big]

(4.8)

\displaystyle\leq K_{V}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}~|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p-1}~\frac{|X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{u,x_{j}}|}{N}\Big]

(4.9)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\Big(\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]\Big)+\frac{K}{N^{p-1}}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big],

(4.10)

where in (
4.8
), we used the
symmetrization trick
again. (
4.9
) follows from Assumption
1
(2) and (
4.10
) is a consequence of Young’s inequality, where
\varepsilon
is some positive constant which can be chosen to be arbitrarily small. Note that one can choose
\varepsilon,K
to be positive constants (both independent of
s,t,N
) satisfying
\varepsilon\in(0,\lambda-\lambda_{1})
for any
\lambda_{1}\in(0,\lambda)
.
Thus, we conclude that

\displaystyle e^{\lambda_{1}p(s-t)}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq p\int_{t}^{s}e^{\lambda_{1}p(u-t)}\bigg((\lambda_{1}+\varepsilon-\lambda)\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]+\frac{K}{N^{p-1}}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]\bigg)\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\leq\frac{Kp}{N^{p-1}}\int_{t}^{s}e^{p(\lambda_{1}-\lambda)(u-t)}\mathrm{d}u\leq\frac{K}{N^{p-1}(\lambda-\lambda_{1})},

where we noted that
\varepsilon
can be chosen to be arbitrarily small, such that
\lambda_{1}+\varepsilon-\lambda
remains negative (so the summation term can be upper bounded by zero).
We used (
4.7
) to bound
\mathbb{E}\big[|X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{u,x_{j}}|^{p}\big]
and then noted that
\int_{t}^{s}e^{p(\lambda_{1}-\lambda)(u-t)}\mathrm{d}u\leq 1/p(\lambda-\lambda_{1})
to conclude the result.

Therefore, for all
\lambda_{1}\in(0,\lambda)
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{p}\Big]\leq\frac{K}{N^{p-1}}e^{-\lambda_{1}p(s-t)}.

∎

The following proposition provides
L^{2}
-estimates for the differences of the processes defined in (
4.1
) with the same initial points, but at different starting times. The results are used in Section
6.1
to establish time-regularity estimates for the derivatives of the function
u
.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let Assumption
2
hold. Consider the first variation process with components
(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}})_{s\geq t\geq 0}
defined by (
4.1
) for
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and assume that the starting positions
x_{i}\in L^{4}(\Omega,\mathbb{R})
are
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable random variables that are identically distributed over all
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
.
Then there exist

\lambda_{2}\in(0,\min\{\lambda-2K_{V},\lambda_{1}\})
,
\lambda_{3}\in(0,\min\{\lambda-2K_{V},\lambda_{2}\})
, and

K>0
(all independent of
s,t,T,N
) such that for all
T\geq s\geq t\geq 0

with
s-t<1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq K(s-t)e^{-2{\lambda_{2}}(T-s)},

(4.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{K(s-t)}{N}e^{-2\lambda_{3}(T-s)}.

(4.12)

Proof.

Note that in the following proof, the positive constant
K
is independent of
s,t,T,N
and may change line by line.

Part 1: Preliminary manipulations.

Similar to the calculations in the proof of Lemma
4.2
, we derive that (recalling that (
4.1
) is an ODE with random coefficients), for all
i,j\in\{1,\dots,N\},~T\geq s\geq t\geq 0
,
\lambda_{2}\in(0,\min\{\lambda-2K_{V},\lambda_{1}\})
,

\displaystyle e^{2\lambda_{2}(T-s)}

\displaystyle|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}|^{2}=|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{2}+2\lambda_{2}\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{2\lambda_{2}u}|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}|^{2}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle-2\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{2\lambda_{2}u}\Big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\cdot\Big(\nabla^{2}U(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u})X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-\nabla^{2}U(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u})X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle-2\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{2\lambda_{2}u}\Big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}})

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}})\Big)\mathrm{d}u.

Therefore, using (
2.2
), we have

\displaystyle e^{2\lambda_{2}(T-s)}|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}|^{2}\leq|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{2}+2(\lambda_{2}-\lambda)\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{2\lambda_{2}u}|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}|^{2}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{2\lambda_{2}u}\Big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\cdot\Big(\nabla^{2}U(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u})X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-\nabla^{2}U(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u})X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\mathrm{d}u

(4.13)

\displaystyle\quad-2\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{2\lambda_{2}u}\Big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\cdot\bigg(~\Big(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}})

\displaystyle\qquad-R^{i,t,s}_{s+u}\Big)+\Big(R^{i,t,s}_{s+u}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}})\Big)~\bigg)\mathrm{d}u,

(4.14)

where we added and subtract the following auxiliary term:

\displaystyle R^{i,t,s}_{s+u}\coloneqq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\left[\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}})\right].

The result in (
4.2
) and the fact that the starting positions
x_{i}
are identically distributed, yield that for all
i,j\in\{1,\dots,N\},i\neq j
,
\lambda_{1}\in(0,\lambda)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{i}}\big|^{4}\Big]\leq Ke^{-4\lambda_{1}u},\qquad\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{4}\Big]\leq\frac{K}{N^{4}}e^{-4\lambda_{1}u}.

(4.15)

Part 2: Establishing (
4.11
).
We further estimate the term involving
\nabla^{2}U
, (
4.13
): under Assumption
1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[

\displaystyle\Big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\cdot\Big(\nabla^{2}U(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u})X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-\nabla^{2}U(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u})X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}K\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|~\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}\big|~\big|X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|\Big]}

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{2}\Big]+K\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}\big|^{4}\Big]\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{4}\Big],}

(4.16)

where we employed Young’s inequality (with constants
\varepsilon,K>0
independent of
s,t
and
N
) and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality. We further bound (
4.16
)
by applying Lemma
A.2

(
4.16
)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+K(s-t)e^{-2\lambda_{2}u}\bigg(\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{s,x_{j},j,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{4}\Big]}+\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{4}\Big]}\bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{2}\Big]+K(s-t)e^{-2\lambda_{2}u}\Big(e^{-2\lambda_{1}u}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}e^{-2\lambda_{1}u}\Big)

(4.17)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{2}\Big]+K(s-t)e^{-4\lambda_{2}u},

(4.18)

for some
\lambda_{2}\in(0,\min\{\lambda-2K_{V},\lambda_{1}\})
, where we injected the estimate (
4.15
) and used that the processes
(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s})_{s\geq t\geq 0}
for
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
are identically distributed (due to the assumption in
4.3
on the starting positions
x_{i}
being identically distributed over
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
) in the second inequality.

As for the term involving
\nabla^{2}V
, (
4.14
), after taking the expectation and summing over
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}})-R^{i,t,s}_{s+u}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big)-\big(X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big)\Big)\cdot\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\Big(\big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}\big)-\big(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}\big)\Big)\Big]\leq 0,

(4.19)

where we once again use the
symmetrization trick
and that
\nabla^{2}V(x)\geq 0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Similar to the analysis involving
\nabla^{2}U
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\cdot\Big(R^{i,t,s}_{s+u}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}})\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}K_{V}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|~\big|(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})-(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})\big|

\displaystyle\quad\cdot~\big|X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}\big|\Big]\leq\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{2}\Big]+K(s-t)e^{-4\lambda_{2}u},

(4.20)

where we applied similar calculations as in (
4.17
) and (
4.18
). After taking the expectation, summing over
i\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
, and injecting our established estimates (
4.18
), (
4.19
) and (
4.20
), we have for an arbitrary small
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle e^{2\lambda_{2}(T-s)}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]+K(s-t)\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{(2\lambda_{2}-4\lambda_{2})u}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad+2(2\varepsilon+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{2\lambda_{2}u}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]\mathrm{d}u.

(4.21)

Further notice that for the first summation term of (
4.21
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg|\int_{0}^{s-t}\Big(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X_{t+u}^{t,x_{i},i,N}-X_{t+u}^{t,x_{l},l,N})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{t+u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{t+u,x_{j}})

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\nabla^{2}U(X_{t+u}^{t,x_{i},i,N})X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{t+u,x_{j}}\Big)\mathrm{d}u\bigg|^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq K(s-t)\int_{0}^{s-t}\bigg(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|\nabla^{2}V(X_{t+u}^{t,x_{i},i,N}-X_{t+u}^{t,x_{l},l,N})|^{2}~|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{t+u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{t+u,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|\nabla^{2}U(X_{t+u}^{t,x_{i},i,N})|^{2}~|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{t+u,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]~\bigg)~\mathrm{d}u

(4.22)

\displaystyle\leq K\big(4K_{V}+\lambda\big)\int_{0}^{s-t}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{t+u,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]\mathrm{d}u

(4.23)

\displaystyle\leq K(s-t)\int_{0}^{s-t}e^{-2\lambda_{1}u}\mathrm{d}u\leq K(s-t),

(4.24)

where we used Jensen’s inequality in (
4.22
), Assumption
1
in (
4.23
) and Lemma
4.2
with
\lambda_{1}\in(0,\lambda)
to establish (
4.24
). Consequently, for all
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
,
we have (by choosing
\varepsilon
arbitrarily small)

\displaystyle e^{2\lambda_{2}(T-s)}\sum_{i=1}^{N}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\quad~\leq K(s-t)+K(s-t)\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{-2\lambda_{2}u}\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+2(2\varepsilon+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{2\lambda_{2}u}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big].

Using
\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{-2\lambda_{2}u}\mathrm{d}u\leq{1}/{(2\lambda_{2})}

and
(2\varepsilon+\lambda_{2}-\lambda)<0
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq K(s-t)e^{-{2\lambda_{2}}(T-s)}.

(4.25)

This concludes the first part of the statement (
4.11
).

Part 3: Establishing (
4.12
).
Mimicking the estimates in (
4.8
)–(
4.10
), we first establish a result to deal with the term involving
\nabla^{2}V
:

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}})-R^{i,t,s}_{s+u}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2N}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\sum_{l=1,l\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big)-\big(X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big)\Big)\cdot\Big(\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}+X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}\big)\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\cdot\nabla^{2}V(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{j},l,N}_{s+u})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\cdot\Big(\big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}\big)-\big(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{j},j,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}\big)~\Big)\Big]

(4.26)

\displaystyle\leq K_{V}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|\Big)\cdot\Big(\frac{1}{N}\big|X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{j},j,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}\big|\Big)\Big]

(4.27)

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{2}\Big]+\frac{K}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{j},j,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{2}\Big],

(4.28)

where we once again used the
symmetrization trick
in (
4.26
),
1
(2) in (
4.27
) and Young’s inequality with constants
\varepsilon,K
chosen such that
\varepsilon<\lambda
in (
4.28
). Similarly, we have

\displaystyle-\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\Big)\cdot\Big(R^{i,t,s}_{s+u}-\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}})\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\sum_{l=1,l\neq j}^{N}K_{V}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|~\big|(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})-(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{s+u})\big|

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 99.58464pt\cdot\big|X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{l},l,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}\big|\Big]

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}K_{V}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|~\big|(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{s+u})-(X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u}-X^{s,x_{j},j,N}_{s+u})\big|

\displaystyle\quad\hskip 85.35826pt\cdot\big|X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{j},j,N}_{{s+u},x_{j}}\big|\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\sum_{l=1,l\neq j}^{N}\Big(\varepsilon\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{2}\Big]+\frac{K(s-t)}{N^{2}}e^{-4\lambda_{2}u}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\Big(\varepsilon\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{2}\Big]+\frac{K(s-t)}{N^{2}}e^{-4\lambda_{2}u}\Big)

(4.29)

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon\Big(\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}\big|^{2}\Big]~\Big)+\frac{K(s-t)}{N}e^{-4\lambda_{2}u},

(4.30)

where we used the moment bounds established in (
4.15
) to get (
4.29
) and applied similar calculations as in (
4.18
).
Taking summation over
i,i\neq j
and collecting the estimates in (
4.28
) and (
4.30
),

\displaystyle e^{2\lambda_{3}(T-s)}

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]\leq\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle+2(\lambda_{3}+2\varepsilon-\lambda)\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{2\lambda_{3}u}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]\mathrm{d}u

(4.31)

\displaystyle+\frac{2K}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{T-s}e^{2\lambda_{3}u}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{j},j,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{j},j,N}_{s+u,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+\frac{K(s-t)}{N}\int_{0}^{T-s}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}e^{(2\lambda_{3}-4\lambda_{2})u}}\mathrm{d}u.

(4.32)

Note that (
4.31
)
\leq 0
since
\lambda_{3}<\lambda
and
\varepsilon
can be chosen to be arbitrarily small, so that this term remains negative. Implementing a crude upper bound (
4.25
) and using that
\lambda_{3}<\lambda_{2}
(hence the integrals remain bounded as
T
gets large), we have

\displaystyle\eqref{eq: 1/N^2 sum term}\leq\frac{K^{2}(s-t)}{N(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})}+\frac{K(s-t)}{2N(2\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{3})}.

Joining together the terms and estimates terms, we have

\displaystyle e^{2\lambda_{3}(T-s)}\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}

\displaystyle-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{T,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1,i\neq j}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Big[|X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}-X^{s,x_{i},i,N}_{s,x_{j}}|^{2}\Big]+\frac{K(s-t)}{N}.

(4.33)

To analyze the summation term in (
4.33
), we first provide the following estimate: For all
u\geq 0
and
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[

\displaystyle\bigg|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{l=1}^{N}\nabla^{2}V(X_{t+u}^{t,x_{i},i,N}-X_{t+u}^{t,x_{l},l,N})~(X^{t,x_{i},i,N}_{t+u,x_{j}}-X^{t,x_{l},l,N}_{t+u,x_{j}})\bigg|^{2}\bigg]
